## Taiwan's band: radio regions (RF-AS923, 2026-10-23)

Built from RF-AS923-1 (support AS923 as Taiwan uses it, 920-925 MHz, alongside EU868, with a region the field picks once). Research checked on 2026-10-23 and cited in RFC 0001 Amendment 12: NCC LP0002 (edition of 2020-07-01) section 5.8, read in full; LoRa Alliance RP002-1.0.4; The Things Network's frequency plans; Meshtastic's region table; Heltec's V4 pages and Meshtastic's V4 power measurements; Semtech's SX1261/2 datasheet. In software and host tests (C, Python, Dart, browser); nothing has been on air, and nothing is certified. All open; RF-AS923-3, RF-AS923-4, RF-AS923-8 and RF-AS923-13 are **for the maintainer**.

| # | Question | Answer chosen | Why | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| RF-AS923-2 | How does a box learn the field's region, and how is it kept from transmitting outside it? | Two locks. The firmware is built for one region (Kconfig `ATALAIA_RADIO_REGION`: EU868, the default, or AS923-TW; `docker_fw.py ... --region=as923-tw`) and knows only that region's channels, SF, bandwidth and limits; and the field's region travels in every join (key 18, RF-AS923-7). A box refuses a join of another region before anything changes (`BOX_JOIN_REGION`, "another radio region's game"; a radio invite answered `refused`); the modem and the I/O pod refuse another region's CONFIGURE, SET_CHANNEL and MEASURE (`bad_params`). Not a field setting a box would take over the air, and not several regions in one image. | The board (Heltec V4.3, 863-928 MHz) is the same in both bands, but the antenna and the labelling are not, and a product's legality is per build: a box must never be talked into another band by a card, a QR code or a radio frame. Several regions per image (a list in Kconfig) is a small change later if the maintainer wants dual-band boxes. | open |
| RF-AS923-3 | **For the maintainer:** on what legal basis do the boxes transmit in Taiwan, and what is missing? | LP0002 5.8.1, "other IoT devices", 920-925 MHz, as **digital modulation**: LoRa at 500 kHz, whose 6 dB bandwidth must be at least 500 kHz, with at most 8 dBm in any 3 kHz (0.5 W over 500 kHz is about 4.8 dBm per 3 kHz). Not hopping (5.8.1.5 (1): at least 10 channels, 0.4 s each): one shared channel per game cannot hop. Not 5.8.2 (any other device): 50 mV/m at 3 m is about -1 dBm EIRP, below what the V4's front-end module gives at the SX1262's lowest setting, so the firmware would refuse every channel (RF-AS923-9). **Missing:** NCC type approval for anything sold or deployed in Taiwan; a lab measurement of the 6 dB bandwidth (LoRa at 500 kHz is accepted as digital modulation under FCC 15.247's identical 500 kHz rule, but that is not a Taiwanese result); LP0002's 2024 edition, in force since 2024-02-02, was not checked (secondary sources say it changed mainly 6 GHz). | The only reading that gives a single channel useful power; the project page and the console say the region is not certified. | open |
| RF-AS923-4 | **For the maintainer:** which power and airtime in Taiwan? | **0.5 W peak at the antenna port** (5.8.1.2 (1) (B), outdoors), less the antenna gain above 6 dBi (5.8.1.2 (3)): 13 dBm from the SX1262 with the Kconfig defaults (14 dB FEM, 2 dBi). Not the 1 W allowed indoors or at a "special site" (a closed area with controlled access, indoors or out), although a fenced airsoft field might qualify: that reading is the maintainer's. **10% airtime per rolling hour**, this project's own: LP0002 sets no duty cycle and no listen-before-talk for these devices. | Outdoors is the usual field; the conservative limit needs no argument about the site. The 10% is the budget the game's traffic was designed and tested for in EU868 (g2), so a Taiwanese game behaves the same and leaves the band to others. The Heltec V4's output is not linear in its setting (Meshtastic measured about 27 dBm at setting 18), so the Kconfig FEM gain of 14 dB is conservative (it asks for less); E20's conducted measurement (RF-5) applies here too. | open |
| RF-AS923-5 | Which default channel and candidates in Taiwan? | **921.5 MHz**; candidates 920.5 to 924.5 MHz every 500 kHz (nine), each 500 kHz channel inside 920-925 MHz with at least 250 kHz to spare (legal centres 920.25-924.75 MHz). Those overlapping LoRaWAN AS923-1's default channels (923.2, 923.4 MHz) or Meshtastic's TW default are marked `lorawan_default`, as EU868's are. All nine are full power and 10%, so a scan's suggestion and a random draw always give full power. | RP002-1.0.4 lists Taiwan under AS923-1, whose 923.2 and 923.4 MHz every AS923 device joins on; TTN's Taiwanese plan (AS923-925) uses 923.2-924.6 MHz; Meshtastic's TW LongFast is 923.875 MHz at 250 kHz (computed from its slot rule: the djb2 hash of "LongFast" modulo 20 slots, checked against its US default, 906.875 MHz). The lower half of the band avoids all of them (RF-4's lesson: the EU868 default shares 869.525 MHz with Meshtastic). RFID readers may use 920-928 MHz anywhere; the scan shows them. | open |
| RF-AS923-6 | Which LoRa settings in Taiwan? | **SF9 at 500 kHz** (CR 4/5, the same 8-symbol preamble and private sync word). | At SF9, a 500 kHz symbol lasts 1.024 ms, as an SF7 symbol at 125 kHz does: the same preamble time, the same CAD timing, a shorter packet (47 bytes: 77 ms instead of 92; 255 bytes: under 400 ms) and a sensitivity within about 2 dB, so the range should be close to EU868's at the same power. SF7 at 500 kHz would lose about 6 dB of sensitivity. The region table carries SF and bandwidth per region, so this is one row. | open |
| RF-AS923-7 | How does the region travel in the join payload? | **Key 18**, the region's number (1 = AS923-TW), left out for EU868; 0 written, an unknown number, text or anything else is a body error; the channel must fit in the region the key names. All three codecs; 3 valid and 8 invalid shared vectors. The codecs give it by name (`eu868`, `as923-tw`). The Dart table lives in `app/lib/protocol/radio_region.dart` (the codec needs it, and `protocol` does not import `controller`). | EU868 joins stay byte for byte the same; a reader from before skips key 18 and checks the channel against EU868, so an old box refuses a Taiwanese join too (fail closed). A number keeps the payload small inside 448 bytes. | open |
| RF-AS923-8 | **For the maintainer:** may the region change at the field? | Yes, in the GM console's radio settings (GM only, after a confirmation), only with no live game and no scan, as the channel (CH8); `[radio] region` is the default until then. A change moves the field to the region's default channel, **unconfirmed** (the first-setup reminder shows again), and forgets the last scan. The console warns when the USB modem is built for another region (IDENT `region`); the controller does not retune it then, and the modem would refuse the channel anyway. | A field picks its region once; making it a console setting keeps one controller image for both bands. A wrong pick cannot put a box on the air outside its band (RF-AS923-2), only stop the game from starting, which the warning explains. Locking it in the config file only would be safer but less usable. | open |
| RF-AS923-9 | A channel whose power limit the SX1262 cannot keep? | **Refused** (`atalaia_tx_power_for`, `radio_limits_set_channel`; the simulator's model raises): if the conducted power the limit allows is below the SX1262's -9 dBm, the channel may not be used. Before, the power was clamped up to -9 dBm, which could exceed the limit (EU868's 25 mW sub-bands with a front-end module above about 25 dB, or any 5.8.2-style limit). The old `atalaia_eu868_tx_power_dbm` stays for its callers and tests. | Fail closed: transmitting over the legal limit is the worse error. With the Kconfig defaults nothing changes in EU868 (0 and 13 dBm). | open |
| RF-AS923-10 | How does the controller know the modem's region? | The modem's IDENT gains `"region"`; a modem without it is an EU868 build. `GET /api/v1/radio` gives `modem_region` and `region_mismatch`; a phone controller reads its I/O pod's IDENT the same way (no key: EU868). | The controller can explain why a game will not configure (the modem's `bad_params`) before a GM wonders. Old modems need no update to keep working in EU868. | open |
| RF-AS923-11 | The Field Box's Wi-Fi setup page offered "US915", "AU915" and "AS923" (phase 1). | It now accepts only the region the firmware is built for (EU868 or AS923-TW) and says so; the stored value was never used by the radio. | A setup page must not suggest a box can be moved to another band. | open |
| RF-AS923-12 | Which regions might come next, and does the design hold? | Not built. **US915** (FCC 15.247, 902-928 MHz): like Taiwan, a single channel that does not hop needs a 6 dB bandwidth of 500 kHz or more (DTS), with at most 8 dBm in 3 kHz (about 26 dBm in practice) and 1 W conducted; or 50-channel hopping at 0.4 s each; 15.249's alternative is 50 mV/m at 3 m. So US915 would be another SF9/500 kHz row with a conducted limit and its own default and candidates. **AU915** (ACMA LIPD class licence, 915-928 MHz): digital modulation up to 1 W EIRP with at most 25 mW per 3 kHz (whether a 500 kHz minimum applies to that item is unclear), or 20-channel hopping; an EIRP row. The table already has the limit kind (ERP, EIRP, conducted, with the free antenna gain), SF and bandwidth per region and a dwell limit per region (enforced by the radio limits and the simulator when not 0; 0 in both regions today). US915 and AS923-TW overlap at 920-925 MHz, which is why the join carries the region and not just the channel. | The maintainer asked for a table, not if/else; the two candidates fit it without new code paths, and both need their own legal check (the sources are listed in RFC 0001 Amendment 12 and here). | open |
| RF-AS923-13 | **For the maintainer:** which antenna and which board for Taiwan? | The same Heltec V4.3 (the high-power version covers 863-928 MHz; Heltec sells it as an 863-870 or a 902-928 MHz option) with an antenna made for 915-925 MHz, not the 868 MHz whip: a mistuned antenna loses range and reflects power into the front end. Its gain counts against the 0.5 W only above 6 dBi, and Kconfig `ATALAIA_RADIO_ANTENNA_GAIN_DBI` must be set to it. To be bought and measured (the E20 conducted measurement, RF-5) before a Taiwanese field. | Hardware and purchasing are the maintainer's; the firmware cannot tell which antenna is fitted. | open |
| RF-AS923-14 | What changes inside the radio driver at 500 kHz, and what is unchecked? | SetModulationParams takes the region's SF and bandwidth (code 0x06 for 500 kHz, low-data-rate optimisation off: 1.024 ms symbols); before every transmission bit 2 of register 0x0889 is cleared at 500 kHz and set otherwise (SX1261/2 datasheet 15.1, "modulation quality with 500 kHz LoRa bandwidth"); the CAD detection peak is 23 at SF9 (22 at SF7) with a minimum of 10, from Semtech's CAD application note, **to be checked at bring-up** like the rest of the CAD settings (CH10). The TIME beacon's fixed 40 ms airtime estimate (`FIELD_LINK_AIRTIME_MS`) is left as it is: a TIME packet is a few milliseconds shorter in AS923-TW. | Facts from the datasheet; nothing measured on a 500 kHz channel yet. | open |

## Flaky browser tests: the GM console and the mode editor (2026-10-23)

The GM console's browser tests (`controller/console/tests/`), and later one of the mode editor's (`simulator/editor_tests/`, FL-3), failed now and then under load. Each failure was traced to its race; a test that waited for the wrong thing was fixed in the test, and a race a GM could hit was fixed in the console and is listed here. The others, fixed in the tests only: the German test picked its language on the login page while the login was still under way (the logged-in view replaced that picker before its change was heard), and the check-in test's *card seen* box is ticked from the controller's answer, so Playwright's `check()` could find it unticked after a re-render (as the levels box of commit f4dace2).

| # | Question | Answer chosen | Why | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FL-1 | **A console race (product fix):** why did the Check-in tab sometimes show no list and no *Export CSV*, for good? | The tab never reloaded when the live game changed. `componentDidUpdate` compared the game in the previous props with the current one, but those props hold the App itself, which already has the new game, so the two were always equal. Opened before the stream's first message had told the page the game (a slow controller, a `#token=` link straight to the tab), the tab loaded for "no game" and stayed so; with the Check-in tab open while the GM ended a game and made the next, it kept the last game's players. Now the panel reloads whenever the live game's id differs from the one it last loaded for (a new game, the first one heard, the game's end), clears the last game's list at once, and keeps only the latest load's answer (an earlier load can answer last). A browser test opens the tab with no game, then makes one, ends it and makes another, without leaving the tab (it fails on the old panel every time). | Seen in the tests under CPU load (2 of 13 runs on one or four busy CPUs); a GM on a slow link or a busy controller hits the same order. The fix is in the console only: the controller's answers were right. | maintainer (2026-10-23): accepted as recommended, to review later |
| FL-2 | **A console race (product fix):** why did *Search* on the field-map page sometimes send two Nominatim requests less than a second apart (the test measured 0.67 s)? | **The throttle timed its second with the wall clock.** `PlaceSearch` (`components/place-search.js`) kept the last request's time from `Date.now()` and slept the rest of the second with `setTimeout`, which runs on the monotonic clock; a step of the wall clock between two searches (a time sync, a virtual machine catching up, the user setting the time) shortened the wait by the step. Seen in the test containers: in one run `Date.now()` moved 205 ms more than `performance.now()` over one second, and one run sent the third request 0.74 s after the second on the browser's own clock. Now the page times it with `performance.now()`. The test measured on the wrong clock as well (the route handler's `time.monotonic()`, which also counts how late Playwright got round to each request): it now reads the browser's monotonic time at the moment the page hands each Nominatim request to `fetch` (an init script wraps `fetch`), asserts one `fetch` per request the route saw (nothing cached was sent), and steps the wall clock 0.9 s forward between the two searches; with the old page it fails every time (0.14 s), with the new one it passed 10 of 10 full-suite runs under a busy loop on all 32 CPUs. No sleep added. | The policy's "an absolute maximum of 1 request per second" (MF-44) holds whatever the wall clock does; the test proves the page's own sending times rather than Playwright's scheduling. | open |
| FL-3 | **A mode editor race (test fix, and a product fix beside it):** why did `test_each_line_of_the_examples_knows_its_block` (`simulator/editor_tests/`) fail once under load with `54 != 81` (master_satellites, node)? | **The test read two generations.** Choosing an example fetches `examples/<name>.json` and only then loads it; the test waited for `local function` in the code pane, which the example before it (Hold-All, 54 lines in `node.lua`) already showed, so under load it took Hold-All's line map and then, in a second call, master_satellites' script (81 lines) once its file came. `lineBlocks` itself is consistent (it maps the script it is given and returns null when the two disagree). Fixed in the test: the editor now sets `body[data-example]` to the example's name once it is loaded and shown (empty while one is on its way, and after *New* or *Open*), every test that chooses an example waits for it (`choose_example`), and the line-map test reads the map and the script in one call. **Fixed in the editor**, found on the way: two examples chosen quickly loaded whichever file came last, possibly the first one chosen; now only the one chosen last loads, and *New* or *Open* drops an example still on its way (`editor.js`, a counter). New test `test_an_example_chosen_before_another_does_not_replace_it` (holds Hold-All's file with Playwright's `route` until Domination is shown; fails without the counter). | Waiting for the real end state, not for text that two states share; no sleeps. Proof: 10 runs of the whole editor suite (33 tests) with a container busy-looping 48 processes on the machine's 32 CPUs (24 s a run instead of 14 s): 10 of 10 passed. | open |

## Firmware versions that carry their commit (2026-10-23)

The maintainer approved (2026-10-23) that every development build carries its commit, so that it is known exactly what a box runs, e.g. `0.3.0-dev.412+g8560d54` (the next version, the commits since the last release tag or since the start, the short hash); a real release (0.3.0, tag `v0.3.0`) comes later with the maintainer's go-ahead. Nothing is tagged yet. All open; FV-1, FV-2, FV-3, FV-4 and FV-7 are **for the maintainer**.

| # | Question | Answer chosen | Why | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FV-1 | **For the maintainer:** where does "the next version" live? | **In `core/include/atalaia/version.h`, the project's single version**: `ATALAIA_VERSION_MAJOR/MINOR/PATCH` say the release being developed (0.3.0) and a new `ATALAIA_VERSION_PRERELEASE` says `"dev"` while it is, so `atalaia_version()` is `0.3.0-dev` (HELLO's `core_version`, the app's settings, the modem's and the pod's logs) and every firmware falls back to it. A release commit empties `ATALAIA_VERSION_PRERELEASE` (`0.3.0`) and is tagged `v0.3.0`; the commit after it sets the next numbers and `"dev"` again. `firmware/esp32/CMakeLists.txt` and `tools/docker_fw.py` read the header; `core/CMakeLists.txt`'s `project(VERSION 0.3.0)` follows its numbers (CMake keeps no pre-release). | `0.3.0-dev` sorts after `0.2.0` and before `0.3.0` (Semantic Versioning section 11), so a tree without history still orders correctly. A separate NEXT field would be a second number to keep in step, and the core would go on saying 0.2.0 while it carries unreleased changes (the Unreleased section is long). | open |
| FV-2 | **For the maintainer:** the commit in the version string, or in a separate field? | **In the version string, as Semantic Versioning build metadata** (`+g8560d54`): allowed wherever a firmware's version is parsed or compared and **ignored in every comparison**: the box (`fw_update.c`: the offer, the release text, `not_newer`), the controller's firmware list and updates, the site server's catalogue, `tools/firmware_release.py` (`firmware_release.py`'s new `firmware_version_key`; `version_key` stays strict), the GM console's `compareVersions`, `docker_fw.py --fw-version`, CMake's check. 1-20 of `0-9A-Za-z.` after `+`, no empty identifier, the whole version at most 35 characters (an image's at most 31). **Mode packages refuse it** (`fw_version_valid_plain` on the box, `version_key` in Python): a mode's version names its archive and registry entry, where two equal versions must not differ. Shared vectors: `protocol/vectors/firmware.json` (`build_metadata`, new `order` and `equal` entries, the signed release `dev_build`; the invalid release text `build_metadata` is gone). | `esp_app_desc_t` has room (`0.3.0-dev.412+g8560d54.dirty` is 28 of 31), HELLO's `fw_version` already takes 31, so **no protocol change** and one text everywhere it shows: the boot log, HELLO, the console's box list and firmware panel, the adoption answer, a box's report and `node.update_failed`, the modem's IDENT. A dev build can be signed with a bench key and installed over the air like a release. A separate field would need a new HELLO key, a second IDENT field and every display taught to join them. Older controllers, site servers and boxes refuse a version with `+` (upload refused, `unknown_version`, `bad_release`): only dev builds carry one. | open |
| FV-3 | **For the maintainer:** what exactly does a build carry, and who works it out? | `tools/docker_fw.py` on the host (the build runs in Docker, where the repository's history may not be readable): `git describe --tags --abbrev=0 --match 'v[0-9]*' --exclude 'v*-*'` for the last **release** tag (a pre-release tag such as `v0.3.0-rc.1` never resets the count), `git rev-list --count <tag>..HEAD` (or `HEAD`: since the first commit; 164 today), the first 7 hex digits of HEAD (always 7: no length that changes with the repository), and `.dirty` when `git status --porcelain --untracked-files=no` lists anything (untracked files do not count). Version: `<core>.<count>+g<hash>[.dirty]` while the core's version is a pre-release; on a release tree, exactly `0.3.0` on its tag with no change, otherwise `0.3.0+<count>.g<hash>[.dirty]` (equal to the release in the order: bump version.h right after a release). No git, or no commit (a tarball, the verify script's fresh `git init` with nothing committed): the core's version, `0.3.0-dev`. `--fw-version=` keeps an exact version (a release or a bench test). Every variant gets it now (modem and pod too); `docker_fw.py` prints it before and after the build, and `docker_fw.py version` prints it alone. A plain `idf.py build` (CI's `esp32-build` jobs, a manual build) gets `0.3.0-dev` without a commit. | What the maintainer asked; the host has the repository and git, the IDF image may not (a worktree's `.git` points outside the mount, `safe.directory`). Counting since the last release tag gives small numbers that grow along history. | open |
| FV-4 | **For the maintainer:** which development build may update which? | Semantic Versioning order with build metadata ignored, as the box already applied it (anti-rollback by version, decision FU3-*): `0.2.0` < `0.3.0-dev` < `0.3.0-dev.9+g...` < `0.3.0-dev.10+g...` (numbers as numbers) < `0.3.0-rc.1` < `0.3.0`; the next version's dev builds above the release. **Two builds with the same count are equal** (another commit, a dirty tree): the box refuses either way with `not_newer`, the controller does not offer it, the console shows no button; to go sideways, flash by USB. Host tests: `test_job_dev_builds` (`test_fw_update.c`), `test_development_builds` and `BuildVersionTests` (`controller/tests/test_firmware_updates.py`), the console's ordering checked in the browser against the shared vectors. | The box must still refuse "not newer". Caveat: the count is "commits in history", not "newer code": two branches at the same count are equal, and a branch with more commits sorts above another's newer work; dev builds are for the bench and development, a field runs releases. | open |
| FV-5 | The I/O pod's IDENT carries `fw_version` in 23 bytes at most (capability proxy, PF6). | `pod_fw_version` (`main/pod/pod.c`, host-tested): the version as it is when it fits (`0.3.0-dev.412+g8560d54` is 22); else the build metadata loses its last identifiers one by one (`.dirty` first: `0.3.0-dev.412+g8560d54.dirty` gives `0.3.0-dev.412+g8560d54`), then the whole metadata, then the old cut. The pod's boot log has the whole version. | Raising 23 is a proxy protocol change (the 256-byte IDENT budget); the commit matters more than the dirty mark, which the boot log still shows. | open |
| FV-6 | Where does the version show? | The box's boot log (`Atalaia firmware v0.3.0-dev.164+g8560d54.dirty (core v0.3.0-dev)`, as before), the modem's and the pod's boot logs (now with the image's version, not only the core's), HELLO's `fw_version` (the console's box list and firmware panel, untouched: they show the text), the modem's IDENT (the controller's log `modem: {...}`, the console's Game tab), the pod's IDENT (the app shows it as given). The OLED splashes (box and modem) show `v` and the first 20 characters, clipped to the line's width: unchanged here (the box's splash is in `shell.c` and `oled_view.c`, being changed by other work); a short form there (`v0.3.0-dev.164`) is a follow-up. | Every place that already showed `fw_version` now shows the commit with no change; the app and the console parse no version except the console's update offer (FV-2). | open |
| FV-7 | **For the maintainer:** the controller's, the site server's and the app's own versions? | **Unchanged** (controller and site server `0.1.0`, the app's `pubspec.yaml` and settings `0.2.0`; `controllerApiVersion` is an API contract, not a release): they are separate packages that carry no commit. Proposal: the release commit for 0.3.0 sets all of them to 0.3.0 with version.h (a release checklist line). | Scope kept to what the firmware reports, as asked; their versions are not compared anywhere. | maintainer (2026-10-23): release 0.3.0; the controller, the site server and the app are 0.3.0 with it |
| FV-8 | How do the OLED splashes (box and modem) show a version that carries its commit? (FV-6's follow-up) | **Two lines: the version without its commit, then the commit** (`oled_version_lines`, `oled_view.c`, host-tested): `0.4.0-dev.12+gabc1234` shows `v0.4.0-dev.12` and `abc1234`; `.dirty` shows as `*` (`abc1234*`); past a release, `0.3.0+5.g8560d54` shows `v0.3.0+5` and `8560d54`; a release shows `v0.3.0` alone, on one line as before; other build metadata (a bench's `--fw-version=0.3.0+bench`) goes on the second line as it is. The first line is Montserrat 14 in the 92 pixels right of the tower (the tower moved 2 pixels left and the text 4, on both splashes): when `v…` does not fit it drops the `v` (`0.4.0-dev.164`, `oled_version_fit`), and anything longer still ends in `...`. The modem's version line is now Montserrat 14 too (unscii 8 holds 11 characters there; `v0.4.0-dev.12` is 13), its commit line unscii 8; the lines move up when there is a commit. The splashes take the whole version from `esp_app_desc_t` (no more `v%.20s`). References: `splash`, `splash_dev`, `splash_dev_dirty`, `modem_splash`, `modem_splash_dev`, `modem_splash_dev_dirty` (`test/screens/oled/`), and a check that no splash line is clipped or leaves the screen. | The splash has no translated text, so it is the same in every language; the version line is whole with its `v` for every count up to 39 (most up to 99) and whole without it up to 199 (checked for every count, `check_version_widths`); past that some end in `...`, and the commit, on its own line, still says exactly what the box runs. The count since the last release tag stays small (3 today). `*` rather than `.dirty`: `abc1234.dirty` does not fit the modem's line. | open |
| FV-9 | How do CI's firmware images carry their commit? (FV-3's follow-up: a plain `idf.py build` got `0.4.0-dev`) | CI's `esp32-build`, `esp32-modem-build` and `esp32-pod-build` jobs check out the whole history (`fetch-depth: 0`, so the tags and the count are known), mark the checkout safe for the container's root, work the version out with `python3 tools/docker_fw.py version` (the same function as a local build), fail when the clone is shallow or a pre-release came out without a commit, pass it as `-D ATALAIA_FW_VERSION=…`, and check the image's `project_version` (`project_description.json`) is it. `docker_fw.py` on a **shallow clone** (which cannot count) now gives the core's version with the commit and no count, `0.4.0-dev+g60edef4`, instead of counting only the commits it has (`0.4.0-dev.1+g…`, a wrong number that would order it); on a release tag it still gives `0.3.0`. Tests: `BuildVersionTests` (shallow cases, and a full, a shallow and a shallow-on-a-release-tag clone of a real repository). | One function for local and CI builds. `fetch-depth: 0` costs a few seconds on this repository. Checked locally: on a full clone the step prints `0.4.0-dev.3+g60edef4`, on a `--depth 1` clone `0.4.0-dev+g60edef4` (and CI's `sh -e` stops at the shallow check), in the `espressif/idf:v5.3.2` image as root on a checkout owned by another user; actionlint 1.7.7 reports nothing in these jobs (its two notes are the existing `flutter-test` step's). | open |

## Money Factory (2026-10-23)

The maintainer approved building Money Factory (2026-10-23), the phase 3 mode decision A1 deferred because "collect, then deposit elsewhere" needs state shared between boxes, "which no document decides yet". Built as `modes/money_factory/` (`mode.yaml`, `node.lua`, `field.lua`, seven YAML screens, 10 event-sequence tests), with the Field Box preset, the console's and the app's event lines, Dart goldens, Field Box reference images and the project page; tested in the simulator (`simulator/tests/test_money_factory.py`), through the real controller (`controller/tests/test_money_factory.py`), in Chromium, in Dart and on the host box; not yet on real boxes, real NFC tags or real radios, and Wokwi did not run (R7). The numbering is **MF-A***n*: Money Factory, after A1 (the prefix MF-*n* alone is the field map's). MF-A2, MF-A3, MF-A5 and MF-A6 are **for the maintainer**; the rest are open.

| # | Question | Answer chosen | Why | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| MF-A1 | How are these decisions numbered? | MF-A1 on: "MF" for Money Factory and "A" for decision A1, whose blocker they answer. MF-0 to MF-49 stay the field map's (2026-10-22/23); a search for `MF-A` finds only these. | The task suggested MF-A1; plain MF-*n* would have collided with the map's 50 decisions, and continuing A14 on would put a 2026-10-23 decision in plan phase A's section. | open |
| MF-A2 | **For the maintainer:** A1's blocker: where does the state shared between boxes live, the controller's `field.lua` or tags rewritten by the boxes? | **The controller's `field.lua`** keeps each player's wallet (what they carry), adds `money.collected`, takes off `money.deposited` (never below 0) and tells the boxes that need it (the mode command `wallet`: `player`, `amount`, `n`). The tags stay read-only. | Tag modes already need a controller (A4: no game key, no valid tag), so this adds no new requirement; NFC writing is not built on the box (PF15) nor in the app's prop shell, a written tag would need its own signed format and a longer tap, and a player could keep an old tag copy with money on it. The Medic Kit's `field.lua` (MK) already shares per-player counts this way. | open |
| MF-A3 | **For the maintainer:** one `node.lua` for two roles that react to the same input (an NFC tag): how does a node know whether it is a factory or a bank? | A node is a **factory until `field.lua` tells it it is a bank** (the mode command `bank`): at the game's start, within a second for a bank that joins late, again every 30 s (a lost CMD), and after it rebooted. No core, FFI or firmware change. **Alternative:** `node.role()` in the script API (M5-5's review item: the core, the simulator, the Dart bridge and the firmware runner), which would make the command and its reminders unnecessary. | M5-5 and M6-6 avoided a `node.role()` because the inputs or a setup command already said what a node does; here the inputs do not, and a command per bank every 30 s is a few bytes of airtime against a box's STATE every 15 s. Until told, a bank shows a free factory, so a GM sees a bank that was never told. | open |
| MF-A4 | The rules. | **Factories** are captured as in Domination (`capture_hold_ms` 3000, the GM's owner override, `zone.captured` / `zone.neutralized`, the OLED's `neutral`/`captured`). While a team holds one it makes `production` (1) every `tick_s` (10, on the game's clock, as Domination's tick) up to `capacity` (50); a team that takes it over gets the money in it. A player of the holding team takes **all it holds, up to what they may still carry** (`carry_max` 20; 0: no limit). With `capture` off every factory always makes money and anyone collects. **Banks** take all a player carries, from any team at any bank, and score that much for the player's team on the bank box (E6: STATE carries the gain). One unit of money is one point. | Domination's capture is what players know; "take it all" needs no extra buttons; a carrying limit makes players run back and forth, a takeover that keeps the money is worth fighting for, and points on the bank box need no new scoring path. | open |
| MF-A5 | **For the maintainer:** what if a box misses the controller's words? | Wallets travel as absolute amounts with a counter `n` per player, so a box ignores an older or repeated word, and its own collection or deposit counts at once until the next word. A box out of reach goes on with what it knows; when its events arrive, the controller adds or takes them off. **Known limit:** a player could bank the same money at two banks while one of them is out of reach, or carry a little over `carry_max` across factories (simulator test `test_a_bank_out_of_reach_goes_on_with_what_it_knows`). | The same trade-off as the Medic Kit's limit across kits (MK): a box keeps playing without the controller. Making a bank ask the controller first would stop deposits whenever the radio is slow. | open |
| MF-A6 | **For the maintainer:** carried money and being hit. | With the Medic Kit in the same game (`org.atalaia.money_factory+org.atalaia.medic`), a player **healed at a kit loses what they carry** (`drop_on_heal`, on by default; `field.lua` hears `medic.heal`). Without kits, a hit player keeps it (no event says who was hit). | The game needs risk on the way to the bank; a heal is the one event that says a player was hit. A house rule ("a hit player drops the money") cannot be checked by the boxes. | open |
| MF-A7 | How does a game end? | With `target` above 0, the first team whose money banked at every bank reaches it wins (`game.win` from `field.lua`); 0 (the default) leaves the end to the game's time. `state.field.banked` (each team's banked money) reaches the console's field state. | As VIP / Payload's `end_on_extract`; a timed game is the usual default. | open |
| MF-A8 | Is Money Factory a Field Box built-in preset? | Yes, the **fourteenth** (label "Money Factory", the fifth entry of the picker's second page), with the manifest's defaults: a box can only join a controller's game for a mode it has (K10, M5-6), and fetched packages need mode keys (P5E-4). Picked on its own a box is a factory that captures and makes money, but refuses tags (`no_game`). Its seven templates are in the image. On a Reduced box the script, 8.3 KB, takes about 36 KB of the 64 KB Lua heap once loaded, and about 54 KB composed with Codes, close to the M4-8 bound of 56 KB, as the Hacking Terminal with Codes (`test_scripts_fit_a_preset_and_a_reduced_box`); a Full box has room to spare. ESP-IDF builds with no warnings; the Field Box image grows by 18,400 bytes, for the script and seven templates (0x1bf560 before, built from a clean copy of the commit before, 0x1c3d40 after; 12 % of the 2 MB OTA slot free, 0x3c2c0 bytes). Wokwi did not run (R7). | The other tag modes are presets too (A4); the image has room. | open |
| MF-A9 | The Android prop, the GM console and the app. | **No code change** for the prop: the app's catalogue reads the manifest (`builtin_modes.g.dart`, regenerated), a phone with NFC can be a bank, and a factory with NFC and its on-screen team buttons. The console lists the mode from the controller's catalogue (there are no built-in console presets: the *Presets* tab holds the GM's own); the console (`feed.events`, four languages) and the app (`event.money.*`, four languages) say both events in words. | Every built-in mode reaches the console and the app through its manifest. | open |
| MF-A10 | Screens and languages. | The templates choose Portuguese when the box's language is `pt` and English otherwise (the box's `lang`, P5H-7), without a `lang` parameter of their own (the Medic Kit's and VIP's predate the box's language); refusals are a `reason` with its words in the template. `neutral`, `captured` and `final_score` are reused so the box's OLED shows a free zone, the holder with the time held, and the end. The manifest has `i18n.pt` only, as every mode (P5H-7, P5K-1: no `i18n.de` or `i18n.zh-tw` yet), so German and Chinese boxes and consoles show its English texts; the project page, the console's and the app's event lines and the glossary have all four (German "Money Factory", as German players use English mode names; Chinese 印鈔廠; both AI-drafted, *check*). | One place for each text; the box's own language reaches the templates already. | open |
| MF-A11 | Events. | `money.collected` (33) and `money.deposited` (34) as the schema has them since phase 3 plan phase A (`player`, `amount`, the team in the common field); no new event or data key. A wallet lost to a heal, the reminders and refusals are not events (a log line, or the screen). | ADR-P3-8's events, unchanged; refusals are not events in the other tag modes either. | open |
| MF-A12 | What is left out? | Banks that serve one team (needs parameters per node), stealing from a factory or a carrier, a player's wallet in the app's player view, a factory's money surviving its reboot in a field game (as the Medic Kit's charges, MK15), German and Chinese manifest texts (MF-A10), and real hardware (NFC on the PN532 and phones, radios). | Each needs a design, a protocol change or the hardware. | open |

## The project page's roadmap headlines (site/, 2026-10-23)

The maintainer found the roadmap section out of date: the per-phase texts were current, but the intro, each phase's one-line summary (`short`), the status tags and the main page's status line still described 2026-10-20 (the first board playing Bomb, phase 4 "started", phase 5 "started"). Brought in line with README.md's roadmap table, each `docs/phase-*/STATUS.md` and CHANGELOG [Unreleased]; only the headlines changed (`roadmap.intro`, `roadmap.p1..p5.short`, `hero.status`, `try.board.text`, the tags in `site/src/roadmap.html` and `index.html`), in all four languages.

| # | Question | Answer chosen | Why | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| SITE-1 | Which tag does each phase get now? | Phase 0 "Done"; phases 1 and 2 "Software done" (unchanged); phase 3 stays "In progress" and the lit one (`class="now"`); phases 4 and 5 go from "In progress" to **"Software done"**. | SP1's meaning holds: "Software done" says every software plan phase is built and the gate waits for what software cannot give. Phase 4 has its 22 software plan phases built (left: a real server with a domain, the legal review P4-6 before hosted mode goes live, HW1-HW4); phase 5 its 13 (left: creating the registry repository, the native review of German and Chinese, the carrier PCB, build photos and community content: maintainer, reviewers and hardware). Phase 3 still has software in progress (the phone controller's slices S7b and S7c, iOS), so it stays lit. The tag never claims a gate: each phase's gate line still says what it waits for, and the STATUS.md files keep "In progress" for the phase as a whole. | open |
| SITE-2 | What do the headlines say about the bench? | Real Heltec V4.3 boards play whole games with the field controller (invites over the radio, captures, pairing by code, updates over the air, a missing mode fetched; the OLED's hold bar, points and held time; box screens in four languages); the I/O pod's firmware has not run on a board yet. | What the bench sessions of 2026-10-20 to 10-23 showed (decisions RJ, BN, OT, FV and the bench commits); "the first board plays Bomb" was three days old. | open |

## The I/O pod relays field and pair frames (2026-10-23)

Follow-up PE24-91 of slice S7c (the phone as field controller with only an I/O pod for a radio): the pod relayed plain radio packets but no field frames and no pair frames, so such a phone could not send sealed invitations and re-invites, end a game with GAME_OVER (`protocol/field-frames.md`) or adopt a box by its code (`protocol/adoption.md` section 7). Built in the pod firmware, the core's proxy codec, the Python reference codec, the shared vectors and the protocol documents; the phone's side (`app/`) is S7c's. In software and host tests (the pod's own code against an in-memory phone, and the host pod the app's tests run), not yet on a pod with a phone.

| # | Question | Answer chosen | Why | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PE24-100 | **For the maintainer (a protocol addition):** how does the pod carry field and pair frames? | Three radio frames, the modem's FIELD_TX, FIELD_RX and FIELD_ACK (`0x09`-`0x0B`) **plus 3**, as every other radio frame: **RADIO_FIELD_TX `0x0C`** (phone → pod: id uint16 BE + a whole frame), **RADIO_FIELD_RX `0x0D`** (pod → phone: the frame heard + rssi + snr) and **RADIO_FIELD_ACK `0x0E`** (pod → phone: id + status 0-8), payloads unchanged. Pair frames (type 10) have no frame types of their own on the modem: they ride FIELD_TX and FIELD_RX, told apart by their header's type, and so they do here (no PAIR_TX/RX types). The pod runs the modem's code (`pod.c` now passes the modem's types up to FIELD_ACK and takes `0x0C` from the phone; `atalaia_proxy_modem_type` maps `0x05`-`0x0E`): the shape check (version 1, game 0, type 9 encrypted 31-217 bytes or type 10 in the clear 10-209), `hop_limit` 0 on air, with or without a game, *busy* (8) during a MEASURE, *no_radio* (7) without a chip, *invalid* (4) for another shape, and the radio task's duty-cycle budget and sub-band TX power for every transmission (`radio_task_send_tagged` on the pod as on the modem; the host pod's `radio_limits`, as the host modem's). The pod never holds the field's key and never opens a frame: sealing is the phone's. | The rule "the modem's types plus 3" keeps one parser and the controller's logic on the phone; a pair-specific type would add nothing the header does not say. Within version 1 as RADIO_NOISE was (PF2): an older pod ignores `0x0C` (counted, unanswered), and the phone knows before trying (PE24-101). | open |
| PE24-101 | **For the maintainer (a protocol addition):** how does the phone know a pod relays them, and can measure? | Three **optional IDENT bools**, numbered as the phone's side of slice S7c already reads them (PE24-91; `pod_codec.dart` on main, keys 6 and 7) and with S7a's proposed `measure` (PE24-77, which had suggested key 6) moved to the next free key: **key 6 `field_frames`** (true: it relays RADIO_FIELD_TX/RX/ACK), **key 7 `pair_frames`** (true: and pair frames in them; a reader takes it only with `field_frames`, as the phone does) and **key 8 `measure`** (true: it answers RADIO_CMD MEASURE, i.e. it has a radio chip and a measuring build). This pod firmware always writes all three: `field_frames` and `pair_frames` true (they are the firmware's, as the modem's JSON `"field": true, "pair": true`; without a radio chip FIELD_TX is answered no_radio, and `has_radio` says so), `measure` true only with a radio chip. A pod that leaves them out is an older one: it can measure if `has_radio` (every pod so far could) and relays neither. The C and Python codecs refuse a value that is not a bool (vectors `field_frames_not_bool`, `pair_frames_null`, `measure_not_bool`); readers that do not know a key skip it, as keys 6-23 always were. In C, `atalaia_proxy_ident_t` gains `field_frames`, `pair_frames`, `measure` as `atalaia_proxy_opt_t` (UNSAID, NO, YES), zero meaning left out, so every existing caller encodes as before. | The phone's side landed first with keys 6 and 7; matching it costs nothing and keeps the Dart code as it is. Separate keys rather than one bitmask: they mirror the modem's JSON IDENT word for word; a bitmask would save 4 bytes, not enough to keep four button colours (PE24-102). | open |
| PE24-102 | Does IDENT still fit? | Yes: a fully fitted pod (4 buttons, keypad, ring, audio, NFC, case), battery, the three flags and a 23-byte `fw_version` take **237 bytes** of 256 (231 before; the host test now allows up to 238). Four button colours with the flags take 261 even with a 5-byte version, so the pod leaves the colours out (PF6's compact IDENT) a little sooner than before (three colours still fit with a short version; with a dev build's 22-byte version the colours were already left out). The compact IDENT and the last resort (no capabilities) keep the flags: they are not optional to the phone. | The flags matter more than the colours, which are cosmetic (button N is the N-th team without one). | open |
| PE24-103 | Where do the pod's RADIO_FIELD_RX and RADIO_FIELD_ACK go, and how are they tested? | As RADIO_RX: a synchronous answer (an invalid frame's ACK, a short frame's ERROR) back on the link the FIELD_TX came on; a frame heard and a transmission's outcome to the link that configured the radio, else the link of the phone's last radio frame (its RADIO_CMD IDENT, which every phone sends first); with no phone, heard and counted only. Host tests (`test_pod.c` `test_field_relay`, against the vectors): a field frame sent without a game, its id in the radio tag and its duty-cycle refusal and success back as RADIO_FIELD_ACK, a field frame heard back as RADIO_FIELD_RX, pair frames both ways, an invalid shape, a frame shorter than its id, busy during a MEASURE, a game configured on USB with a second phone on Bluetooth, a Bluetooth-only phone, no phone, no radio, the largest frame (217 bytes) over Bluetooth; IDENT's flags with and without a radio, and the old "unknown type" check now uses `0x0F`. The **host pod** (`test/pod_host`) relays them with no change of its own (it runs `pod.c`) over its UDP air, so the app's end-to-end tests can use a pod where they used the USB modem; its `status` line gains `field_tx` and `field_rx`. The shared vectors' new frames and flags are in their own `pod_relay` section of `capability_proxy.yaml` (5 radio frames from `field_frame.json`'s `announce_minimal` and `pairing.json`'s OFFER, 2 IDENTs, 3 invalid IDENTs): the C tables (`proxy_vectors.h`, by `protogen.py`) and the Python test read them with the rest, while the Dart codec test, which throws on a radio type it does not know, keeps passing until S7c reads them too. | One routing rule for every radio frame; a section of its own lets the firmware land before the app without breaking the app's tests. | open |
| PE24-104 | **For the coordinator (S7c's Dart side, a4cdc4c):** does the firmware match what the phone reads? | Checked against `app/lib/controller/pod_modem.dart`, `controller_radio.dart` and `app/lib/pod/pod_codec.dart` on main. **Matching:** frame types `0x0C` RADIO_FIELD_TX (id uint16 BE + frame, `sendField`), `0x0D` RADIO_FIELD_RX (frame + rssi int8 + snr int8; the phone needs at least 3 bytes), `0x0E` RADIO_FIELD_ACK (exactly 3 bytes: id + status, named by `txStatusNames`: 0 ok, 1 duty cycle, 2 timeout, 4 invalid, 6 queue full, 7 no radio, 8 busy); IDENT key 6 `field_frames`, key 7 `pair_frames` (bools, true from this firmware; the phone sets `hasPairFrames` only with key 6 true too). **Differences, none breaking:** (1) key 8 `measure` is new; `pod_codec.dart` skips it (keys 8-23 unknown) and `PodModem.canMeasure` still takes `has_radio`; reading it (an `old_firmware` refusal when false, PE24-77) is a later Dart change; (2) the firmware writes `measure: false` on a pod without a radio, and the Dart encoder writes only true values; decoding is unaffected; (3) `pod_codec.dart` takes any value for keys 6 and 7 (`== true`), while C and Python refuse a non-bool: a Dart test of the three new invalid vectors would need `decodeIdent` to check the type; (4) `app/test/pod/codec_test.dart` does not read the new `pod_relay` section of `protocol/vectors/capability_proxy.yaml` (`ident`, `radio`, `invalid`); it should, and its `_ident` helper should pass `field_frames`/`pair_frames` (the radio vectors need cases for types 12-14 in its switch, which throws on an unknown type; that is why they are in a section of their own). | The two sides meet on these bytes; the remaining items are Dart-only and optional for the relay to work. | open; items (1), (3) and (4) done as PE24-105 |
| PE24-105 | The phone's side of the pod's new IDENT flag and frames (PE24-104's Dart items). | **Done in `app/`.** `pod_codec.dart`: `PodIdent.measure` (key 8, an optional bool: null when a pod leaves it out, written as the pod writes it, `false` included, and given to the controller as the modem's JSON word `measure`); `decodeIdent` refuses a value of keys 6, 7 or 8 that is not a bool, as C and Python do (before, any value but `true` was read as false); `radioFieldTx`, `radioFieldRx` and `radioFieldAck` build the three payloads (`PodModem.sendField` uses the first). `PodModem.canMeasure` is `has_radio` and not `measure: false`: a pod that says it cannot measure makes the channel scan refuse up front with `old_firmware` (503, the Pi's reason for a modem without `measure`, in `scan_unavailable` too), no MEASURE sent; a pod without the flag (older firmware) is taken to measure, as before. `app/test/pod/codec_test.dart` reads the `pod_relay` section of `capability_proxy.yaml` (its two IDENTs both ways, its five radio frames, types `0x0C`-`0x0E` in the shared switch, built and read back from the frame, its three invalid IDENTs) and its `_ident` helper passes the three flags; `controller_channel_test.dart` checks `measure: true`, then `false` from a scripted pod. | The firmware writes the flag since PE24-101; reading it costs a line and makes the phone refuse a scan the way the Pi does instead of failing it on the pod's `unknown_cmd`. | open |

## One scenario compiler, in the core (PE24-109 to PE24-117, 2026-10-23)

PE24-84 had ported the Pi's scenario validator and compiler (Python, about 1,330 lines and the 450-line runtime) to Dart for the phone (about 3,800 lines), kept alike only by the shared vectors, so every rule change was made twice. The maintainer approved (2026-10-23) one implementation: the validator and the compiler in Lua, run by the shared C core, which both controllers already load. Done in one pass, validator and compiler both. PE24-109, PE24-110 and PE24-113 are **for the maintainer**.

| # | Question | Answer chosen | Why | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PE24-109 | **For the maintainer (a licence and a layout):** what moves into the core, and what stays with each controller? | **The rules and both files move; reading the text stays.** `core/scenario/` holds the validator (`validator.lua`), `field.lua`'s writer (`luagen.lua`), `mode.yaml`'s content and its emitter (`package.lua`, `yaml.lua`: PyYAML's `safe_dump` for the documents a scenario makes, written for that subset rather than ported whole), the names and types (`manifest.lua`), the Python semantics the rules were written with (`py.lua`: equality, truth, `str()`/`repr()` for paths and messages, a float's shortest `repr`, `json.dumps`'s length), the radio size of the scenario events from the core's protocol tables (`protocol.lua`), the Unicode classes (`unicode.lua`, generated) and the runtime (`runtime.lua`, moved from `controller/atalaia_controller/scenarios/`). Each controller keeps its reader (the Pi's PyYAML loader, the phone's PyYAML-compatible one: YAML 1.1, a key given twice, surrogate pairs, impossible dates) and hands the core what it read as the **manifest's data tree** (core/scenario/README.md): every value typed as `scenario_compile.json`'s `loads` already wrote them (`{"str"}`, `{"int"}`, `{"float"}`, `{"bool"}`, `{"null"}`, `{"timestamp"}`, `{"list"}`, `{"map"}` with any key), plus `{"alias": n}` for one object met twice (a YAML anchor; a list that holds itself) and `{"other"}` for what PyYAML alone builds (`!!set`, `!!binary`). Gone: `scenarios/lua.py` and the Python validator's and package's logic (thin wrappers remain: `validator.py`, `package.py`, `compiler.py` with the encoder), and the Dart `scenario_validator.dart`, `scenario_lua.dart`, `scenario_runtime.g.dart` and the rules, emitter and Python semantics of `scenario_manifest.dart` and `scenario_package.dart`. **Licence:** the compiler's code is now the core's, GPL-3.0-or-later; it was AGPL-3.0-or-later on the Pi (P4R21) and GPL-3.0-or-later in the app's port (PE24-84). | Reading YAML 1.1 as PyYAML does is a parser per platform (Dart's on package:yaml, the Pi's PyYAML); putting one in the core would be a third, larger one. Everything after reading is one implementation. The core is GPL and ships in the app, as the port already did; the controller's server code stays AGPL. | maintainer (2026-10-23): GPL-3.0-or-later is fine for the compiler in the core (the controller and site server stay AGPL) |
| PE24-110 | How do the controllers call it, and does the Field Box pay for it? | **`atalaia/scenario.h`:** `atalaia_scenario_check`, `atalaia_scenario_compile`, `atalaia_scenario_literal` (a value as a Lua literal, for tests) and `atalaia_scenario_free`: the data tree's JSON in, a malloc'd JSON answer out (`{"ok", "problems"}`, with `"id"`, `"version"` and `"files"` when it compiles; `{"error"}` for a text that is not a data tree, a caller's bug). Each call runs in a Lua state of its own (no state between calls, so any thread may call): base, string, table, math and utf8 only, no `load`, `dofile` or `require`, a memory limit and an instruction limit (PE24-113). The Lua sources are embedded by the build: `core/cmake/embed_scenario.cmake` writes them into a C file in the build folder with CMake alone (Gradle's NDK build needs no Python), whenever one changes; a module is loaded only when a call needs it. **Option `ATALAIA_SCENARIO`** (on by default); the Field Box's firmware lists the core's sources itself and never builds it, so its images keep their size. Python: `Core.scenario(op, tree)` in `atalaia_sim/core.py` (looked up on first use, so a core without it still loads); Dart: `AtalaiaCore.scenario` in `app/lib/ffi/bindings.dart` (not a leaf call: a compile takes up to about a tenth of a second). The app's NDK check now also looks for the scenario symbols. | One call per check; the core already crosses both bridges with JSON. | open |
| PE24-111 | Must the move change any problem or any package? | **No: byte for byte.** Every case of `protocol/vectors/scenario.json` (6 valid, 60 invalid) and `scenario_compile.json` (28 manifests, 42 loader cases) gives the same paths, codes, levels and files on both controllers; regenerated with the core's compiler, `scenario_compile.json` changes only in its description. The messages too: 130,000 generated manifests (mutations of the vectors' manifests and examples: values of every kind anywhere, keys that are not texts, aliases, dates, big integers, NaN, exotic Unicode in every text, 27,000 that compile with descriptions that exercise the emitter's quoting and folding) gave the same problems, messages included, and the same files as the Python compiler. Three details are kept as the Python wrote them: a float's `repr` (shortest digits, `1e-05`, `-0.0`); the Unicode classes of Python 3.12 (Unicode 15.0.0, now one table for both controllers, which ends PE24-84's "newer Unicode" gap); and a polygon's area summed as Python 3.12's `sum()` sums floats (Neumaier's compensation; the Dart port had summed plainly, so a polygon with an area within rounding of the 1e-12 limit could differ). | The vectors were the contract; the generated manifests check what they do not cover. | open |
| PE24-112 | Manifests that made the Pi's validator raise (a 500), found by the generated manifests. | **Problems now, as for any other bad value.** A `lang` that JSON cannot write (a list holding a date): the setup's size check called `json.dumps` and raised; now the size check is skipped for it and `bad_lang` says what is wrong. A dependency cycle through about 1,000 objectives or more: the message's path finder recursed past Python's limit; the Lua one does not. An integer too large for a float in a destination (`lat: 10**400`): `math.isfinite` raised; now `bad_destination`. | Input must never crash the check (PE24-107, PE24-108). | open |
| PE24-113 | **For the maintainer:** how much may one check take? | **64 MB and 200 million Lua instructions per call; past either, the problem `too_complex`** ("the manifest is too large or too complex to check: it needs more memory or time than the compiler may take"). The largest manifest that compiles (64 objectives with 1,200-byte descriptions in two languages, 133 KB) takes about 4 MB and 14 million instructions, 0.12 s on a PC; the worst found within 256 KB, 1,500 objectives in one dependency cycle (each named with the whole cycle: 18 MB of messages; the Pi raised RecursionError), would take 113 MB: it stops at the memory limit after 2.6 s on a PC, `too_complex`. A data tree nested more than **100 levels** deep is `bad_manifest` ("the manifest is nested more than 100 levels deep"), whatever key holds it (the Python reported the unknown key, or raised RecursionError past about 300 levels; a real manifest is under 10). | A phone's controller answers the radio and the console from the same isolate; one request must not hold it for long. The limits are 15 times what a real manifest needs. | open |
| PE24-114 | And without the core library? | **The manifest is read but not checked or installed.** The Pi: `POST /api/v1/scenarios/check` answers `ok: false`, no problems and `checked_in_core: false` (as the site server already did); `POST /api/v1/scenarios` is 503 (Unavailable); `python3 -m atalaia_controller.scenarios validate` now needs the core too (exit 2 without it; `--no-core` only skips loading the generated `field.lua`). The phone: the same answers (`ControllerError.unavailable`). The site server: unchanged (it reads, and refuses to save, `core_unavailable`); a staff page's package is shown only with the core. Python's `validate`, `compile_scenario` and `lua_literal` take `core=`, else load the default library once (`ATALAIA_CORE_LIB`), and raise `CoreUnavailable` without one. | Every controller, the site server's image and CI build the core; a validator without it would be the second implementation again. | open |
| PE24-115 | What happens to the tooling of PE24-84 and PE24-88? | `tools/scenario_compile_vectors.py` writes the vectors with the core's compiler (the Pi's reader) and no longer writes `scenario_runtime.g.dart`; `app/tool/check.sh` no longer copies `runtime.lua`. New: `tools/scenario_unicode.py` (`--check` in `tools/check.sh` and CI's generated-files job), `core/tests/test_scenario.c` (ctest `scenario`, also under ASan and UBSan: the entry points, the event sizes against the core's codec for ids of 100 to 200 bytes, the limits, 30 malformed trees and 3,000 mutated ones), StyLua on `core/scenario/` (`.styluaignore` skips the generated table). | One place to change, one place to check. | open |
| PE24-116 | **For the maintainer:** the generated files' header names the compiler's old home. | Kept: `field.lua` and `mode.yaml` still say "Generated by the Atalaia scenario compiler (controller/atalaia_controller/scenarios)", so that every package compiles byte for byte as before (a package's hash is what a standby and the site server compare). Changing it to `core/scenario` is one line in `luagen.lua` and `package.lua` and new vectors, when wanted. | Byte-identical packages were the brief. | open |
| PE24-117 | What does the move cost in speed? | A check is a fresh Lua state that parses the compiler's sources (about 110 KB) each time: about 2 ms for a small manifest and 3 to 6 ms for a compile on a PC (Release; three times that in a Debug build), against under 1 ms in Python; the largest manifest that compiles takes 0.12 s (Python 0.08 s), and reading a 230 KB text with PyYAML (0.7 to 4.7 s) still costs more than checking it. Lua's bytecode differs by platform, so the sources are not precompiled. | Fast enough for an editor that checks as the GM types; one state per call keeps calls independent and safe. | open |
